Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The picture for the sample test. [Image]

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where vertices are these points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (largest subset of vertices where every pair is connected by an edge)

## Key Insight

In a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected by an edge. For points i and j to be connected, we need |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

Instead of thinking about edges, let's think about when two points are NOT connected. Two points are NOT connected if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So for a set of points to form a clique, any two points in the set must satisfy |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate x
points.sort()

# Greedy algorithm to find maximum clique
current_max_sum = -10**18  # will be max of (x_i + w_i) for selected points
size = 0

for x, w in points:
    if x - w >= current_max_sum:
        size += 1
        current_max_sum = max(current_max_sum, x + w)

print(size)
```

View Extracted Code 
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate x
points.sort()

# Greedy algorithm to find maximum clique
current_max_sum = -10**18  # will be max of (x_i + w_i) for selected points
size = 0

for x, w in points:
    if x - w >= current_max_sum:
        size += 1
        current_max_sum = max(current_max_sum, x + w)

print(size)

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where vertices are these points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (largest subset where every pair has an edge)

## Key Insight

In a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected by an edge. This means for any two points i and j in the clique, we must have |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

Let me think about what this condition means:
- If two points are close to each other, their distance might be less than the sum of their weights, so they won't be connected
- If two points are far apart, their distance is likely greater than the sum of weights, so they will be connected

## Key Observation

For a set of points to form a clique, every pair must satisfy the condition. Let's think about the constraint |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to saying that if we have a set S of points, then for any two points i,j in S, the distance between them is at least the sum of their weights.

## Critical Insight

Consider sorting the points by their coordinates. If we have points in order from left to right, for a clique to exist, the condition must hold between every pair.

For two points i and j where x_i < x_j, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This can be rearranged to: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So for any two points i and j in our clique, if x_i < x_j, then x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

This means that if we define a new value for each point: y_i = x_i - w_i, then for any two points i,j in the clique with x_i < x_j, we need y_j ≥ y_i + 2w_i (this seems complex).

No, because if p_i and p_{i+2} have a smaller distance than needed.

## Key Realization

For any two points in the clique, |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

If I sort the points by x-coordinate, and consider a subset of points, the condition must hold for every pair.

The most restrictive constraint will be between the closest points. If the distance between the closest two points in a set is sufficient, then all other pairs will have larger distances.

Therefore, if I have a set of points, the condition is equivalent to: the distance between any two points is at least the sum of their weights.

But since the distances increase as we go further apart, if consecutive pairs satisfy the condition, then all pairs might satisfy it due to the distance property.

Actually, let me think about the minimum distance requirement. If I have three points A, B, C where x_A < x_B < x_C, and I require |A-C| ≥ w_A + w_C, |A-B| ≥ w_A + w_B, |B-C| ≥ w_B + w_C.

If the consecutive pairs satisfy the condition, does it guarantee the non-consecutive pair? 

|A-C| = |A-B| + |B-C| ≥ (w_A + w_B) + (w_B + w_C) = w_A + 2w_B + w_C > w_A + w_C

So |A-C| > w_A + w_C, which means |A-C| ≥ w_A + w_C holds.

Therefore, if all consecutive pairs in a sorted sequence satisfy the distance constraint, then all pairs in that sequence will satisfy the constraint.

This means that any subset of points that forms a sequence in sorted order where consecutive pairs satisfy |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j will form a clique.

## Algorithm

1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point, we want to find the longest sequence where consecutive pairs satisfy the constraint
3. This becomes a dynamic programming problem: we can use a greedy approach or DP to find the maximum clique

Actually, we can simplify. Since any valid clique must have points in sorted order, and consecutive pairs need to satisfy the constraint, we can:

- Sort points by x-coordinate
- Use DP where dp[i] = maximum clique size ending at point i
- For each point i, check all previous points j where x_i - x_j ≥ w_j + w_i
- dp[i] = max(dp[j] + 1) for all valid j, or 1 if none found

## Final Insight

The key insight is that we can form a clique if and only if for every pair of points in the clique, their distance is at least the sum of their weights.

After sorting by x-coordinate, we can use DP where:
- dp[i] = maximum clique size that can include point i
- For each point i, we check all j < i where x_i - x_j ≥ w_i + w_j
- dp[i] = max(dp[j] + 1) for valid j, or 1

But this is O(n²).

We need to optimize. The condition x_i - x_j ≥ w_i + w_j is equivalent to x_i - w_i ≥ x_j + w_j.

Define for each point: value_i = x_i - w_i, and value'_i = x_i + w_i.

Then the condition becomes: value_i ≥ value'_j.

So for point i to be connected to point j (j < i), we need value_i ≥ value'_j.

For a clique, if we have points p_1, p_2, ..., p_k in sorted order, then we need:
- value_{p_2} ≥ value'_{p_1}
- value_{p_3} ≥ value'_{p_2}
- ...
- value_{p_k} ≥ value'_{p_{k-1}}

This means the sequence must satisfy that each value_i ≥ value'_{previous}.

We want to find the maximum k.

We can use DP with coordinate compression and binary search, or simply use a data structure.

Actually, since the constraint is chain-based, we can simulate greedily:

1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point, compute value = x - w and value_prime = x + w
3. Use DP to find maximum chain where value_i ≥ value_prime_j for consecutive pairs

But O(n²) might be too slow.

Wait, the condition value_i ≥ value_prime_j means that if we process points in increasing order of x, and we maintain the maximum chain length ending at each value_prime, we can do better.

Actually, we can use this approach:
- Sort by x-coordinate
- For each point i, we need to find the maximum dp[j] such that value_i ≥ value_prime_j
- We can maintain a data structure that maps value_prime to the maximum dp value for that value_prime
- Then update dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1) for all j where value_i ≥ value_prime_j

We need O(n log n).

## Optimized Approach

We process points in increasing order of x-coordinate.

For each point i, we want to find the maximum chain length ending at a point j < i such that value_i ≥ value_prime_j.

This is equivalent to: among all previous points j, we want max(dp[j]) for all j with value_prime_j ≤ value_i.

We can maintain a data structure (like a segment tree or a Fenwick tree) indexed by value_prime.

Steps:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point i (in order):
   - Query the data structure for max(dp[j]) where value_prime_j ≤ value_i
   - If found, dp[i] = query_result + 1
   - Otherwise, dp[i] = 1
   - Update the data structure with (value_prime_i, dp[i])
3. Answer is max(dp[i]) for all i

But we also need the value_prime_j ≤ value_i condition.

Actually, the condition is value_i ≥ value_prime_j, so value_prime_j ≤ value_i.

Yes.

We can use a segment tree or Fenwick tree that supports range maximum queries and point updates.

Since coordinates can be up to 10^9, we need coordinate compression.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Sort by x-coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    # For each point, compute value = x - w and value_prime = x + w
    # We'll use these for our DP condition
    
    # dp[i] = maximum clique size ending at point i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    # We need to query max(dp[j]) for j < i with value_prime_j <= value_i
    # We'll use coordinate compression and a Fenwick tree for range maximum queries
    
    # Get all value_prime values
    values_prime = []
    for x, w in points:
        values_prime.append(x + w)
    
    # Get all value values (x - w)
    values = []
    for x, w in points:
        values.append(x - w)
    
    # Actually, we don't need to store values separately since we use value_i for the condition
    
    # Sort values_prime for coordinate compression
    sorted_values_prime = sorted(set(values_prime))
    comp_values_prime = {}
    for idx, val in enumerate(sorted_values_prime):
        comp_values_prime[val] = idx + 1  # 1-indexed
    
    # Build Fenwick tree for range maximum queries
    # Size = len(sorted_values_prime)
    size = len(sorted_values_prime)
    
    # Fenwick tree for maximum queries (not sum, so different)
    fenw = [0] * (size + 1)
    
    def fenw_max_update(pos, val):
        while pos <= size:
            fenw[pos] = max(fenw[pos], val)
            pos += pos & (-pos)
    
    def fenw_max_query(pos):
        res = 0
        while pos > 0:
            res = max(res, fenw[pos])
            pos -= pos & (-pos)
        return res
    
    max_clique = 1
    
    for i in range(n):
        x, w = points[i]
        value_i = x - w  # y_i
        value_prime_i = x + w  # z_i
        
        # Query for max dp[j] where value_prime_j <= value_i
        # value_prime_j <= value_i means we need value_prime_j <= value_i
        # Find position in sorted_values_prime where value_prime_j <= value_i
        # Since value_i = x - w, and values are not necessarily in sorted_values_prime
        # We need to find the largest value_prime_j that is <= value_i
        
        # Binary search in sorted_values_prime for the largest value <= value_i
        left, right = 0, len(sorted_values_prime) - 1
        pos_query = 0
        while left <= right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if sorted_values_prime[mid] <= value_i:
                pos_query = mid + 1  # +1 because we want 1-indexed position
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid - 1
        
        # If pos_query == 0, no points satisfy the condition
        if pos_query == 0:
            dp[i] = 1
        else:
            # Query max dp for positions 1 to pos_query
            max_val = fenw_max_query(pos_query)
            dp[i] = max_val + 1
        
        # Update the Fenwick tree at position corresponding to value_prime_i
        pos_update = comp_values_prime[value_prime_i]
        fenw_max_update(pos_update, dp[i])
        
        max_clique = max(max_clique, dp[i])
    
    print(max_clique)

solve()
```

Wait, I think I have an error in the condition. Let me double-check.

The condition is: |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

For i > j (since sorted by x), x_i - x_j ≥ w_i + w_j, which gives x_i - w_i ≥ x_j + w_j.

So we need value_i ≥ value_prime_j.

For point i to be able to form a clique with point j, we need value_i ≥ value_prime_j.

But in our DP, we want to extend from j to i, so we need value_i ≥ value_prime_j.

However, when we process point i, we query for j < i where value_prime_j ≤ value_i.

This is correct.

But there's a subtle issue: for a clique of size k, every pair must satisfy the condition, not just consecutive ones. But as I reasoned earlier, if consecutive pairs satisfy the condition, then all pairs will satisfy it due to the distance being larger.

So yes, this should work.

Let me test with the example:
- Points: (2,3), (3,1), (6,1), (0,2)
- Sort by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

For (0,2): value = 0-2 = -2, value_prime = 0+2 = 2
dp[0] = 1

For (2,3): value = 2-3 = -1, value_prime = 2+3 = 5
Need value_prime_j ≤ -1. But j=0 has value_prime=2 > -1, so no j. dp[1] = 1

For (3,1): value = 3-1 = 2, value_prime = 3+1 = 4
Need value_prime_j ≤ 2. j=0: 2 ≤ 2 ✓, j=1: 5 > 2 ✗
So only j=0. query for value_prime_j ≤ 2 → only j=0 has value_prime=2 ≤ 2
dp[2] = dp[0] + 1 = 2

For (6,1): value = 6-1 = 5, value_prime = 6+1 = 7
Need value_prime_j ≤ 5. j=0: 2 ≤ 5 ✓, j=1: 5 ≤ 5 ✓, j=2: 4 ≤ 5 ✓
So max(dp[0], dp[1], dp[2]) = max(1,1,2) = 2
dp[3] = 2 + 1 = 3

Max clique = 3 ✓

Perfect! It matches the expected output.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# dp[i] = maximum clique size ending at point i
dp = [1] * n

# Get all value_prime (x + w) values for coordinate compression
values_prime = []
for x, w in points:
    values_prime.append(x + w)

# Coordinate compression for values_prime
sorted_values_prime = sorted(set(values_prime))
comp_values_prime = {}
for idx, val in enumerate(sorted_values_prime):
    comp_values_prime[val] = idx + 1  # 1-indexed

size = len(sorted_values_prime)
# Fenwick tree for maximum queries
fenw = [0] * (size + 1)

def fenw_max_update(pos, val):
    while pos <= size:
        fenw[pos] = max(fenw[pos], val)
        pos += pos & (-pos)

def fenw_max_query(pos):
    res = 0
    while pos > 0:
        res = max(res, fenw[pos])
        pos -= pos & (-pos)
    return res

max_clique = 1

for i in range(n):
    x, w = points[i]
    value_i = x - w  # y_i
    value_prime_i = x + w  # z_i
    
    # Find the largest index in sorted_values_prime such that value_prime <= value_i
    # We need value_prime_j <= value_i
    left, right = 0, size - 1
    pos_query = 0
    while left <= right:
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if sorted_values_prime[mid] <= value_i:
            pos_query = mid + 1  # 1-indexed position
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid - 1
    
    # Query the maximum dp[j] for j with value_prime_j <= value_i
    if pos_query == 0:
        dp[i] = 1
    else:
        max_prev_dp = fenw_max_query(pos_query)
        dp[i] = max_prev_dp + 1
    
    # Update the Fenwick tree at position corresponding to value_prime_i
    pos_update = comp_values_prime[value_prime_i]
    fenw_max_update(pos_update, dp[i])
    
    max_clique = max(max_clique, dp[i])

print(max_clique)
```

points.sort()

# dp[i] = maximum clique size ending at point i

comp_values_prime = {}

size = len(sorted_values_prime)

def fenw_max_update(pos, val):
    while pos <= size:
        fenw[pos] = max(fenw[pos], val)
        pos += pos & (-pos)

def fenw_max_query(pos):

        pos -= pos & (-pos)
    return res

max_clique = 1

    value_prime_i = x + w  # z_i

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where vertices are these points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (largest subset of vertices where every pair is connected)

## Key Insight

For a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected. This means for any two points i and j in the clique, we must have |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

Let me think about when two points are NOT connected. They are NOT connected when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So for points to form a clique, they must all satisfy that any two points are sufficiently far apart.

## Observation

If we sort points by coordinate, we can analyze the constraints more easily. Let's say we have points sorted by x-coordinate: p_1, p_2, ..., p_n.

For any two points i < j, they are connected if x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This can be rewritten as: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if we define for each point i: y_i = x_i + w_i, then points i and j are connected if x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i, which means y_j ≥ y_i.

Wait, let me reconsider this more carefully:

For points i and j (i < j): they are connected if x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j
This is equivalent to: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i

So if we define y_i = x_i + w_i, then points i and j are connected if y_j ≥ y_i.

This means: two points are connected if and only if the point with larger x-coordinate has a larger value of (x + w).

## Clique Structure

For a clique, every pair must satisfy this condition. So if we have a set of points, they form a clique if for any two points in the set, the one with larger x-coordinate has a larger value of (x + w).

This means that if we sort by x-coordinate, then for a set to form a clique, when we look at their (x + w) values, they must be in non-decreasing order.

Actually, let me reframe: if points are sorted by x-coordinate (x_i < x_j), then they are connected if x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j, which means x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if we define y_i = x_i + w_i, then this becomes y_j ≥ y_i.

For a set of points to form a clique, for any two points i < j in the set, we need y_j ≥ y_i.

This means that in the sorted order, the y-values must be non-decreasing.

Actually, let me check if this is correct. If y_j ≥ y_i for i < j, then x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i, which means x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j. Yes, this is exactly the condition we need.

So for any two points i and j with i < j, they are connected if and only if y_j ≥ y_i (where y_i = x_i + w_i).

But for a clique, we need every pair to be connected. So we need that for any two points in the set, this condition holds.

This is equivalent to: in the sorted order, the sequence of y_i values must be non-decreasing.

## Maximum Clique

The maximum clique size would be the largest subset of points such that when sorted by x-coordinate, their y-values (x_i + w_i) are in non-decreasing order.

Actually, more precisely: if we sort points by x-coordinate, then for any two consecutive points in the clique, we need y_j ≥ y_i. But for non-consecutive points, the condition might be automatically satisfied if the sequence is non-decreasing.

Wait, let me verify: if y_1 ≤ y_2 ≤ ... ≤ y_k for k points in a clique, then for any i < j, y_j ≥ y_i, which means x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i, which means x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j. This gives us the required edge.

So yes, the condition is satisfied.

Therefore, the maximum clique size is the maximum number of points we can select such that when sorted by x-coordinate, their (x_i + w_i) values form a non-decreasing sequence.

This is equivalent to finding the longest non-decreasing subsequence of the values (x_i + w_i).

Wait, but actually we need to be careful. The condition is that for any two points in the clique, the one with larger x has larger (x + w). Since we sort by x, this means we need a non-decreasing sequence of (x_i + w_i).

But there's a catch: the points are already sorted by x, so we just need to find the longest subsequence where (x_i + w_i) is non-decreasing.

However, this is not quite right either. Let me reconsider.

Actually, the condition for two points i and j (with i < j) is x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j, which is equivalent to x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So for a set of points to form a clique in sorted order, we need that for every pair (i,j) with i < j, x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

This means that if we look at the sequence of values v_i = x_i + w_i, we need v_j ≥ v_i for all j > i in our selection.

So yes, we need the longest subsequence of v_i values that is non-decreasing.

However, there's a potential issue: if we have points with the same (x + w) value, they might still satisfy the condition.

Let me check: if v_j = v_i, then x_j - w_j = x_i + w_i, so x_j - x_i = w_i + w_j. This satisfies the equality case, so they are connected.

Therefore, we can have equal values.

So the answer is the length of the longest non-decreasing subsequence of the values (x_i + w_i).

## Algorithm

1. Read n points
2. For each point, compute v_i = x_i + w_i
3. Sort the points by x-coordinate (already given, but we need to maintain order)
4. Find the longest non-decreasing subsequence of the v_i values

Wait, but actually we don't need to use dynamic programming for longest non-decreasing subsequence. We can do this efficiently with a simple sweep.

Actually, for the longest non-decreasing subsequence, we can use a greedy approach or a simpler method.

But let's think again: the constraint is that any two points in the clique must satisfy the distance condition. This is equivalent to the non-decreasing property of (x + w).

So we need to find the maximum number of points we can select such that when sorted by x, their (x + w) values are non-decreasing.

This is exactly the longest non-decreasing subsequence problem.

For n ≤ 200,000, we need an efficient algorithm. The longest non-decreasing subsequence can be found in O(n log n) time.

However, there might be a simpler observation. Let me check the sample:

Points: (2,3), (3,1), (6,1), (0,2)
x + w: 5, 4, 7, 2

Sort by x: (0,2): 2, (2,3): 5, (3,1): 4, (6,1): 7

Now we need non-decreasing subsequence of [2, 5, 4, 7]:
- 2 → 5 (increasing)
- 2 → 4 (increasing)  
- 2 → 7 (increasing)
- 5 → 7 (increasing)
- 2 → 4 → 7 (increasing, length 3)

Longest non-decreasing subsequence has length 3.

This matches the expected output.

## Implementation

```python
import sys

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Create array of (x + w) values
    # Sort by x-coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    # Extract the v_i = x_i + w_i values
    v = [x + w for x, w in points]
    
    # Find longest non-decreasing subsequence
    # This can be done greedily or with patience sorting
    # We'll use a simple approach: find the length of the longest non-decreasing subsequence
    
    # Actually, since we want maximum clique, and the constraint is non-decreasing,
    # we can just simulate the longest non-decreasing subsequence
    
    # We can use a greedy approach with a list to maintain the ending values
    # of non-decreasing subsequences of different lengths
    
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    
    # Initialize with first element
    tails = []
    
    for val in v:
        # Binary search for the first element >= val
        left, right = 0, len(tails)
        while left < right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if tails[mid] <= val:
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid
        
        # If left == len(tails), we can extend the sequence
        if left == len(tails):
            tails.append(val)
        else:
            tails[left] = val
    
    return len(tails)

print(solve())
```

Let me verify with the sample:
- Points sorted by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)
- v = [2, 5, 4, 7]
- tails initially empty
- val = 2: left=0, tails=[], so append 2 → tails=[2]
- val = 5: left=0, tails[0]=2≤5, so left=1, append 5 → tails=[2,5]
- val = 4: left=0, tails[0]=2≤4, left=1, tails[1]=5>4, so left=1, set tails[1]=4 → tails=[2,4]
- val = 7: left=0, tails[0]=2≤7, left=1, tails[1]=4≤7, left=2, append 7 → tails=[2,4,7]
- Length = 3 ✓

Perfect!

```python
import sys

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Sort by x-coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    # Extract the v_i = x_i + w_i values
    v = [x + w for x, w in points]
    
    # Find longest non-decreasing subsequence using patience sorting
    tails = []
    
    for val in v:
        # Binary search for the leftmost position where tails[pos] > val
        left, right = 0, len(tails)
        while left < right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if tails[mid] <= val:
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid
        
        if left == len(tails):
            tails.append(val)
        else:
            tails[left] = val
    
    return len(tails)

print(solve())
```

import sys

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Sort by x-coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    # Extract the v_i = x_i + w_i values
    v = [x + w for x, w in points]
    
    # Find longest non-decreasing subsequence using patience sorting
    tails = []
    
    for val in v:
        # Binary search for the leftmost position where tails[pos] > val
        left, right = 0, len(tails)
        while left < right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if tails[mid] <= val:
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid
        
        if left == len(tails):
            tails.append(val)
        else:
            tails[left] = val

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

## Correct Insight

For any two points i < j, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if we define y_i = x_i + w_i, then we need x_j - w_j ≥ y_i.

Since we want this to hold for all i < j, we need x_j - w_j ≥ max(y_i for i < j).

In other words, for a clique starting from some point, we can extend it as long as the new point satisfies x_j - w_j ≥ max(y_i for all previous points in the clique).

## Algorithm

1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each possible clique, greedily extend it by checking the condition
3. Keep track of the maximum size

Actually, I can do this with a simple greedy:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

max_clique = 0
# Try each starting point
for start in range(n):
    # Use the greedy approach: start from each point and extend
    current_clique = [start]
    # The maximum y_i (x_i + w_i) so far
    max_y = points[start][0] + points[start][1]
    
    # Try to extend to points after start
    for i in range(start + 1, n):
        x_i, w_i = points[i]
        # Check if we can add point i
        # We need x_i - w_i >= max_y
        if x_i - w_i >= max_y:
            # Add point i
            current_clique.append(i)
            # Update max_y to include this point
            max_y = max(max_y, x_i + w_i)
        else:
            # Can't add this point, so break since all subsequent points will be worse
            break
    
    max_clique = max(max_clique, len(current_clique))

print(max_clique)
```

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

points.sort()

max_clique = 1
current_clique = []

# Try to build clique starting from each point
for i in range(n):
    # Build clique starting from point i
    clique = []
    max_y = 0  # max of (x_j + w_j) for points in clique
    
    for j in range(i, n):
        x_j, w_j = points[j]
        y_j = x_j + w_j
        
        # Check if point j can be added to the clique
        # We need x_j - w_j >= max_y
        if x_j - w_j >= max_y:
            clique.append(j)
            max_y = y_j  # update max_y
        else:
            # Can't add point j, but since points are sorted, we can't extend further
            break
            
    max_clique = max(max_clique, len(clique))

print(max_clique)
```

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

points.sort()

max_clique = 1

# For each possible starting point
for i in range(n):
    # Start a clique with point i
    current_max_y = points[i][0] + points[i][1]  # y_i = x_i + w_i
    clique_size = 1
    
    # Try to add points j > i
    for j in range(i + 1, n):
        x_j, w_j = points[j]
        y_j = x_j + w_j
        
        # Check if we can add point j to the clique
        # We need x_j - w_j >= current_max_y
        if x_j - w_j >= current_max_y:
            current_max_y = y_j  # update the max y value
            clique_size += 1
        else:
            # We can't add j, and since points are sorted by x, we can't add any later point
            # because if we can't add j, we can't add j+1, j+2, etc. (distance condition is stricter)
            break
    
    max_clique = max(max_clique, clique_size)

print(max_clique)
```

The condition for a clique should be that for every pair (i,j) with i<j, x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j.

When sorting by x-coordinate, we need that for any i < j, x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

So for a set S of points, if we sort them by x-coordinate as p_1, p_2, ..., p_k, then we need x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i for all i < j.

This means that if we define y_i = x_i + w_i, then x_j - w_j >= y_i for all i < j.

This is equivalent to: if we sort points by x-coordinate, then the sequence of (x_j - w_j) values must be non-increasing relative to the y-values.

Actually, no: we need x_j - w_j >= max_{i < j} (x_i + w_i).

And between (0,2) and (6,1): |0-6| = 6 >= 2+1 = 3 ✓

In my algorithm, when starting at 0:
- I can't add (2,3) because it violates the condition.
- But I can add (3,1) because 3-1 = 2 >= 2.
- Then I can add (6,1) because 6-1 = 5 >= 4.

The algorithm works! It processes points in sorted order and adds them if the condition holds.

I had a mistake in thinking that I skip points - I don't skip, I process in order, and the condition ensures compatibility.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

points.sort()

max_clique = 1

# For each possible starting point
for i in range(n):
    # Start a clique with point i
    current_max_y = points[i][0] + points[i][1]  # max of (x_k + w_k) for k in current clique
    clique_size = 1
    
    # Try to add points j > i in sorted order
    for j in range(i + 1, n):
        x_j, w_j = points[j]
        # The condition for adding point j to the clique
        # We need: x_j - w_j >= current_max_y
        if x_j - w_j >= current_max_y:
            current_max_y = x_j + w_j  # update the max y value
            clique_size += 1
        else:
            # Cannot add point j, and since points are sorted by x, we can't add any later point
            # because if j doesn't satisfy the condition with the current max, no later point will
            break
    
    max_clique = max(max_clique, clique_size)

print(max_clique)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i. We form a graph where there's an edge between points i and j if and only if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

We need to find the maximum clique size - a subset of vertices where every pair is connected by an edge.

## Key Insights

1. **When is there NO edge between two points?** When |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j
2. **When is there an edge between two points?** When |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j

For a clique, every pair of vertices must have an edge between them. So in our graph, a clique corresponds to a set of points where every pair satisfies |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

## Analyzing the Condition

Let's think about when two points i and j don't have an edge: when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

For points on a line, we can sort them by coordinate. After sorting, for points with coordinates x_1 ≤ x_2 ≤ ... ≤ x_n, the distance between points i and j is x_j - x_i for j > i.

So point i and j don't have an edge when x_j - x_i < w_i + w_j.

This doesn't directly give us a pattern. Let me think differently.

## Key Observation

For a set of points to form a clique, every pair must satisfy |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

If we have three points a, b, c with x_a ≤ x_b ≤ x_c, then we need:
- x_b - x_a ≥ w_a + w_b
- x_c - x_b ≥ w_b + w_c  
- x_c - x_a ≥ w_a + w_c

Notice that the third condition (x_c - x_a ≥ w_a + w_c) must follow from the first two, since:
(x_c - x_a) = (x_c - x_b) + (x_b - x_a) ≥ (w_b + w_c) + (w_a + w_b) = w_a + 2w_b + w_c > w_a + w_c

So actually, if the pairwise conditions between (a,b) and (b,c) are satisfied, the condition between (a,c) will automatically be satisfied due to the transitive nature of the inequality.

Wait, that's not quite right. Let me reconsider.

Actually, for points a, b, c with x_a ≤ x_b ≤ x_c:
- We need x_b - x_a ≥ w_a + w_b
- We need x_c - x_b ≥ w_b + w_c  
- We need x_c - x_a ≥ w_a + w_c

The third condition is not automatically satisfied by the first two. We need to check if it holds.

But here's the key insight: **The graph has a special structure**. For any three points in any order, if they don't form a clique, it's because of the specific condition.

## Better Approach

Let me think about when two points don't have an edge. They don't have an edge when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So two points have an edge when they're "far enough apart" relative to their weights.

For a clique, we need every pair to be "far enough apart".

Let me try a different insight: **Sort the points by coordinate**. Then for any three points i < j < k, for them to form a clique, we need:
- x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j (distance between i and j ≥ sum of weights)
- x_k - x_j ≥ w_j + w_k (distance between j and k ≥ sum of weights)
- x_k - x_i ≥ w_i + w_k (distance between i and k ≥ sum of weights)

The last condition is actually what matters - if the first two hold, does the last hold?

We have: (x_k - x_i) = (x_k - x_j) + (x_j - x_i) ≥ (w_j + w_k) + (w_i + w_j) = w_i + 2w_j + w_k > w_i + w_k

So yes, if the first two conditions hold, the third automatically holds! This is because we're dealing with distances on a line.

Therefore, for any three points in sorted order, if every pair of consecutive points (in position) satisfies the distance condition, then all pairs satisfy it.

This means that a clique can be found by checking segments of consecutive points in the sorted order.

## Algorithm

1. Sort points by coordinate
2. For each possible starting point, extend as far as possible while maintaining the condition that consecutive points in the sequence satisfy |x_j - x_i| ≥ w_i + w_j
3. Keep track of the maximum size found

Actually, let me think again. The condition is that for two points i and j (in sorted order), we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

For points in order i < j < k, if we have x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j and x_k - x_j ≥ w_j + w_k, then we automatically have x_k - x_i ≥ w_i + w_k (as shown above).

Therefore, we can use a greedy approach:

- Sort points by coordinate
- For each starting position, extend the clique as long as the condition holds for consecutive pairs
- Return the maximum clique size

# In a conflict graph, two points conflict if they are close (distance < sum of weights).

# Another known result: in a set of points on a line, with distance condition, the maximum clique can be found by considering that if three points are in a clique, then they must satisfy the pairwise conditions.

# i=0 (0,2): for j=1: 2-0=2 >= 2+3=5? no. j=2: 3-0=3 >= 2+1=3? yes. j=3: 6-0=6 >=2+1=3? yes. count=2

# Given the time, I'll output a solution that returns 3 for the sample and hope it's correct in general, but that's not right.

# I recall that in some known problems, the maximum clique in such a graph is the size of the longest subsequence where each consecutive pair satisfies the distance >= sum of weights, and the example might be an exception.

# Perhaps there's a mistake in the problem understanding.

# Let me double-check the condition: "edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights"

# So edge exists if distance >= sum of weights.

# For a clique, every pair must have an edge, so every pair must satisfy distance >= sum of weights.

# In the sample, the set {0,2,3,6} with weights (2,3,1,1) works.

# So the only way is to find such a subset.

# Given that n is 200000, we need an efficient algorithm.

# After research, I found that this is a known problem and the solution is to sort the points and then use a greedy algorithm that for each point, extends the current clique only if the condition with the previous point in the sequence is met, and that the maximum clique is the maximum over all starting points of the length of such a sequence.

# Even though it doesn't work for the example, perhaps the example is different.

# Let me check: in the sample, are there three points that pairwise satisfy the condition?

# A(0,2), C(3,1), D(6,1):
# A-C: 3 >= 2+1 = 3 ✓
# A-D: 6 >= 2+1 = 3 ✓
# C-D: 3 >= 1+1 = 2 ✓

# Yes.

# How can we find such a set without checking all pairs?

# Perhaps use the fact that if a set of points forms a clique, then for any two points i and j in it, x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j.

# This is equivalent to x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

# So for any two points i and j in the clique, we must have x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

# Therefore, if we define for each point i: value = x_i - w_i, and for each point j: value = x_j + w_j, then for any two points in the clique, we must have that the value of the right point (x_j + w_j) >= value of the left point (x_i + w_i)? 

# Let's see: from x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j, we have x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

# So if we define for each point i: a_i = x_i + w_i, and b_i = x_i - w_i, then for any two points i,j in the clique with x_i < x_j, we must have x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i, i.e., b_j >= a_i.

# This means that for a set to be a clique, if we sort by x, then for any two points i < j in the set, we must have b_j >= a_i.

# This is not sufficient because it doesn't ensure the condition for all pairs.

# For three points i < j < k, we need:
# - b_j >= a_i
# - b_k >= a_i
# - b_k >= a_j

# So if we have b_k >= a_j, then b_k >= a_i and b_k >= a_j, and also b_j >= a_i, so it seems like if we have b_k >= a_j, and b_j >= a_i, then the conditions are satisfied.

# Let's verify with the example:
# Point (0,2): a_i = 0+2=2, b_i = 0-2 = -2
# Point (2,3): a_i = 2+3=5, b_i = 2-3 = -1
# Point (3,1): a_i = 3+1=4, b_i = 3-1 = 2
# Point (6,1): a_i = 6+1=7, b_i = 6-1 = 5

# The clique is (0,2), (3,1), (6,1) -> indices 0,2,3
# - for (0,2) and (3,1): b_2 = 2 >= a_0 = 2 ✓
# - for (0,2) and (6,1): b_3 = 5 >= a_0 = 2 ✓
# - for (3,1) and (6,1): b_3 = 5 >= a_2 = 4 ✓

# And the pairwise distance conditions are also satisfied.

# So the condition b_j >= a_i for all i < j in the clique is necessary.

# Is it sufficient?

# Given three points i < j < k in sorted order, we need:
# - x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j  => x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i  => b_j >= a_i
# - x_k - x_j >= w_j + w_k  => x_k - w_k >= x_j + w_j  => b_k >= a_j
# - x_k - x_i >= w_i + w_k  => x_k - w_k >= x_i + w_i  => b_k >= a_i

# Since b_k >= a_j and a_j >= a_i (because x_j > x_i, and weights are positive), we have b_k >= a_i.

# Therefore, if b_k >= a_j and b_k >= a_i, and b_j >= a_i, then all three conditions are satisfied.

# For a set of points, if for every pair i < j with i in the set and j in the set, we have b_j >= a_i, then in particular for any j and i with i < j, b_j >= a_i.

# But is this enough for all pairs? For a set of points, if we have for every pair i < j, b_j >= a_i, then for any three points i < j < k, we have b_k >= a_j and b_j >= a_i, and as shown, this implies the conditions are satisfied.

# Therefore, a set of points forms a clique if and only if when sorted by x-coordinate, for every i < j in the set, b_j >= a_i.

# This is equivalent to: when sorted by x, the values b_i must be at least the maximum of a_i for all points to the left.

# Therefore, to find the maximum clique, we can:

# 1. Sort points by x-coordinate
# 2. For each point, compute a_i = x_i + w_i and b_i = x_i - w_i
# 3. Find the longest subsequence where for every i < j in the subsequence, b_j >= a_i

# This can be done with a greedy algorithm: iterate through the sorted points, and for each point, if b_j >= max_{i in current sequence} a_i, then we can include it.

# Algorithm:
# - Sort by x
# - Initialize max_clique = 1
# - current_max_a = -1 (or -inf)
# - For each point in sorted order:
#   - If b_i >= current_max_a, then we can include this point, so current_max_a = max(current_max_a, a_i), and max_clique = max(max_clique, current_length)
#   - Else, skip

# But wait, if we have a point with b_i < current_max_a, then we skip.

# In the example sorted order: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)
# a = [2,5,4,7], b = [-2,-1,2,5]

# i=0: b0 = -2, current_max_a = -inf, -2 >= -inf -> true, so include, current_max_a = max(-inf, 2) = 2, length=1, max_clique=1
# i=1: b1 = -1, current_max_a = 2, -1 >= 2? false -> skip
# i=2: b2 = 2, current_max_a = 2, 2 >= 2 -> true, include, current_max_a = max(2,4)=4, length=2, max_clique=2
# i=3: b3 = 5, current_max_a = 4, 5 >= 4 -> true, include, current_max_a = max(4,7)=7, length=3, max_clique=3

# So max_clique = 3, which matches the expected output.

# Let me verify if this algorithm works for other examples.

# The condition is that for a set to be a clique, we need for every i < j in the set, b_j >= a_i.

# In the sequence we build, when we include a point, we require that b_i >= current_max_a, which is the maximum a_j for any j in the current sequence (j < i).

# This ensures that for every previous point in the sequence, b_i >= a_j.

# And since the sequence is sorted by x, and we only add points when b_i >= max previous a, then for any two points in the sequence, the condition holds.

# Therefore, the algorithm is correct.

# Final solution:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# If only one point, clique size is 1
if n == 1:
    print(1)
else:
    # For each point, compute a_i = x_i + w_i and b_i = x_i - w_i
    a = []
    b = []
    for x, w in points:
        a.append(x + w)
        b.append(x - w)
    
    # Find the longest subsequence where for every i < j, b_j >= a_i
    # We can do this with a greedy algorithm: iterate and keep track of the max a so far
    max_clique = 1
    current_max_a = -10**18  # very small number
    
    for i in range(n):
        if b[i] >= current_max_a:
            current_max_a = max(current_max_a, a[i])
            max_clique = max(max_clique, i + 1)
    
    print(max_clique)
```

Wait, in the loop, when we include a point, we do max_clique = max(max_clique, i+1), which is the size of the current clique.

And we update current_max_a after including the point.

But is this correct? Let me verify with the example:

points: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)
a = [2,5,4,7]
b = [-2,-1,2,5]

i=0: b[0]=-2 >= current_max_a=-inf -> true, so current_max_a = max(-inf,2)=2, max_clique = max(1,1)=1
i=1: b[1]=-1 >= 2? false -> skip
i=2: b[2]=2 >= 2? true, so current_max_a = max(2,4)=4, max_clique = max(1,3)=3
i=3: b[3]=5 >= 4? true, so current_max_a = max(4,7)=7, max_clique = max(3,4)=4

This gives 4, but expected is 3 and we have only 4 points, and the clique should be size 3.

What's wrong?

In the sequence, after including (0,2) then (3,1) then (6,1), we have three points.

After including (0,2) and then (3,1) and then (6,1), the size should be 3, not 4.

The indices are 0,2,3 -> size 3.

In the loop, when i=2 (index 2), we are at point (3,1), and i+1 = 3, so max_clique = 3.

When i=3 (index 3), we are at point (6,1), and i+1 = 4, so max_clique = max(3,4)=4.

This is wrong because we have only 4 points, and the subsequence should be only 3 points.

The issue is that we are including the point at index 3, which is point (6,1), and we are counting the size as i+1, which is 4, but we only have 3 points in the sequence: indices 0,2,3.

The size of the sequence is the number of points included, not the index.

In the loop, when we include a point at index i, the size of the current clique is the number of points we have included so far, which is i+1 only if we include all from 0 to i.

But we skip some points.

The correct way is to maintain the size of the current clique, and when we include a point, we increase the size by 1.

At i=0: size = 1, max_clique = 1
At i=2: size = 2 (points at 0 and 2), max_clique = 2
At i=3: size = 3 (points at 0,2,3), max_clique = 3

So we need to maintain the current size of the clique, not i+1.

We can do:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

if n == 1:
    print(1)
else:
    a = []
    b = []
    for x, w in points:
        a.append(x + w)
        b.append(x - w)
    
    max_clique = 1
    current_max_a = -10**18
    current_size = 1  # size of current clique (number of points included)
    
    for i in range(n):
        if b[i] >= current_max_a:
            # Include this point
            current_max_a = max(current_max_a, a[i])
            current_size += 1
            max_clique = max(max_clique, current_size)
        # else skip, size remains the same
    
    print(max_clique)
```

Wait, at i=0: size becomes 2? should be 1.

We should start at size=1 for the first point, then for each included point after that, increase by 1.

But at i=0, we include the first point, so current_size should be 1.

Then at i=2, we include the next point, so current_size becomes 2.

At i=3, we include, so current_size becomes 3.

So the algorithm is:

- current_size starts at 0
- for each point in order:
  - if b[i] >= current_max_a, then include it, current_size += 1, current_max_a = max(current_max_a, a[i])

But initially, before any point, current_max_a is -inf, so the first point will be included.

After including the first point, current_max_a = a[0]

Then for the next point, if b[i] >= current_max_a, include it.

In the example:
i=0: b[0]=-2 >= -inf -> true, current_size = 1, current_max_a = max(-inf,2)=2
i=1: b[1]=-1 >= 2? false -> skip, current_size=1, current_max_a=2
i=2: b[2]=2 >= 2 -> true, current_size=2, current_max_a = max(2,4)=4
i=3: b[3]=5 >= 4 -> true, current_size=3, current_max_a = max(4,7)=7

max_clique = max(1,1,2,3) = 3

Perfect.

Edge case: n=1
In the loop, i=0: include, size=1, max_clique=1

So it works.

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# If only one point, answer is 1
max_clique = 1
current_max_a = -10**18  # very small number
current_size = 0

for i in range(n):
    if points[i][0] - points[i][1] >= current_max_a:
        current_size += 1
        current_max_a = max(current_max_a, points[i][0] + points[i][1])
        max_clique = max(max_clique, current_size)

print(max_clique)
```

This should work. Let me verify the example step by step.

Example: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

i=0: point (0,2)
- b = 0-2 = -2 >= -10**18 -> true
- size=1
- current_max_a = max(-inf, 0+2)=2
- max_clique = max(1,1)=1

i=1: point (2,3)
- b = 2-3 = -1 >= 2? false -> skip

i=2: point (3,1)
- b = 3-1 = 2 >= 2? true
- size=2
- current_max_a = max(2, 3+1)=max(2,4)=4
- max_clique = max(1,2)=2

i=3: point (6,1)
- b = 6-1 = 5 >= 4? true
- size=3
- current_max_a = max(4,6+1)=7
- max_clique = max(2,3)=3

Output: 3, correct.

Therefore, the solution is correct. The key insight is that for a set of points to form a clique, when sorted by coordinate, for any two points i < j in the set, we must have x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i, which is equivalent to b_j >= a_i. The greedy algorithm finds the longest such subsequence. 

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# If only one point, answer is 1
max_clique = 1
current_max_a = -10**18  # very small number
current_size = 0

for i in range(n):
    # Calculate b_i = x_i - w_i and a_i = x_i + w_i
    x_i, w_i = points[i]
    b_i = x_i - w_i
    a_i = x_i + w_i
    
    if b_i >= current_max_a:
        current_size += 1
        current_max_a = max(current_max_a, a_i)
        max_clique = max(max_clique, current_size)

print(max_clique)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where vertices are these points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (largest subset where every pair is connected)

## Key Insights

The condition |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j means that two points are NOT connected if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

For a clique, every pair of points must be connected, so for any two points in the clique, their distance must be at least the sum of their weights.

## Important Observation

If we have a clique of points, then for any two points i and j in the clique, |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

This means the points in the clique must be "far enough apart" from each other.

## Strategy

Since the points are on a line, we can sort them by coordinate. For a clique, if we have points sorted by x-coordinate, then for any two points in the clique, the distance between them must be at least the sum of their weights.

A key insight: if we have three points i, j, k with i < j < k in sorted order, then we need:
- |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j (for points i,j)
- |x_j - x_k| ≥ w_j + w_k (for points j,k) 
- |x_i - x_k| ≥ w_i + w_k (for points i,k)

But |x_i - x_k| = x_k - x_i, and this must be ≥ w_i + w_k.

Since x_k - x_i = (x_k - x_j) + (x_j - x_i), we have:
x_k - x_i ≥ w_i + w_k
(x_k - x_j) + (x_j - x_i) ≥ w_i + w_k

For this to hold, we need sufficient spacing.

## Key Insight - Greedy Approach

Actually, let me think differently. In this specific graph, the clique condition is very restrictive.

Two points are NOT connected if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j, which means they're "too close" for their combined weight.

A crucial observation: if we sort the points by coordinate, then for three consecutive points, the middle point needs to be far enough from both ends.

But here's a better approach: we can find the maximum clique by checking sequences of points that satisfy the distance condition.

## Further Insight

After working through the problem, I realize that we can use a greedy approach:

1. Sort points by coordinate
2. For each possible starting point, try to extend the clique as far as possible
3. A point can be added to a clique if it maintains the edge condition with all previously selected points

However, there's a more elegant solution for this specific problem type.

## Final Insight

The key insight is that if we sort the points by coordinate, then in any clique, the points must be spaced such that each pair satisfies the distance condition.

Actually, let me reconsider: if we have a set of points in a clique, then for any two points, their distance must be at least the sum of their weights.

But there's a simpler approach: we can use the fact that we can try all possible subsets with greedy selection, but that's too expensive.

## Optimal Strategy

After analyzing the problem structure, the solution is to:

1. Sort points by coordinate
2. For each possible leftmost point, greedily extend to the right while checking if the distance condition holds with all existing points
3. However, this doesn't work directly because we need all pairs to be connected.

The key insight is that the maximum clique can be found by checking sequences where each new point is far enough from all previous points.

Actually, after thinking more deeply, I believe the solution involves checking for valid cliques in a specific way.

## Working Solution

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# Try all possible cliques by checking sequences
max_clique = 1

# For each starting position, try to build a clique extending to the right
for start in range(n):
    # Start with just this point
    current_clique = [start]
    
    # Try to extend from start
    for end in range(start + 1, n):
        # Check if we can add point 'end' to the current clique
        # It must satisfy the condition with ALL points in current_clique
        can_add = True
        
        for idx in current_clique:
            # Distance between points[idx] and points[end]
            dist = abs(points[idx][0] - points[end][0])
            # Sum of weights
            sum_weights = points[idx][1] + points[end][1]
            
            # They must be connected (distance >= sum of weights)
            if dist < sum_weights:
                can_add = False
                break
        
        if can_add:
            current_clique.append(end)
        else:
            # If we can't add this point, break since adding more won't help
            # because the distance requirement becomes stricter
            break
    
    max_clique = max(max_clique, len(current_clique))

print(max_clique)
```

```python
    clique = [i]

        # Check if point j can be added to the current clique
        # It must be connected to ALL points in the current clique
        valid = True

            sum_weights = points[k][1] + points[j][1]
```

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

points.sort()

max_clique = 1

# Try all possible starting positions
for start in range(n):
    # Build clique starting at 'start'
    current_clique = []
    
    for end in range(start, n):
        # Check if we can add point 'end' to the clique
        # Need to check against all previous points in clique
        can_add = True
        
        for prev in current_clique:
            dist = abs(points[prev][0] - points[end][0])
            sum_weights = points[prev][1] + points[end][1]
            
            if dist < sum_weights:
                can_add = False
                break
        
        if can_add:
            current_clique.append(end)
        else:
            # Can't add this point, but we can still try other endings
            # However, once we can't add, we break since adding more won't help
            break
    
    max_clique = max(max_clique, len(current_clique))

print(max_clique)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, input().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Sort by x-coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    # For each point, calculate v_i = x_i + w_i and u_i = x_i - w_i
    # We'll use DP to find the longest chain where for consecutive points i<j, u_j >= v_i
    # Actually, since all pairs need to satisfy the condition, and we've shown that if consecutive pairs satisfy it, all pairs do,
    # we just need to find the longest sequence where for each consecutive pair (i,j), u_j >= v_i
    
    if n == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    v = []  # v_i = x_i + w_i
    u = []  # u_i = x_i - w_i
    
    for x, w in points:
        v.append(x + w)
        u.append(x - w)
    
    # dp[i] = length of longest clique ending at point i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        for j in range(i):
            # Check if point j and point i can be in the same clique
            # They are connected if u[i] >= v[j]
            if u[i] >= v[j]:
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    print(max(dp))

solve()
```

Wait, this O(n^2) solution will be too slow for n = 200,000.

I need to optimize it.

The constraint is that for each point i, we need to find the maximum dp[j] among all j < i such that u[i] >= v[j].

I can use a data structure that supports range maximum queries.

Since v[j] and u[i] can be very large (up to 10^9), I need coordinate compression.

I'll compress the values of v[i] and use a segment tree or Fenwick tree for range maximum queries.

But actually, I can use a simpler approach with coordinate compression and a Fenwick tree for range maximum queries.

However, given the complexity, let me think if there's a simpler pattern.

After more research in my mind, I recall that for this type of problem, the maximum clique can be found using the property that we're essentially looking for the longest sequence where each new element's "left value" >= previous element's "right value".

The optimized solution:

```python
import sys

def main():
    n = int(sys.stdin.readline())
    if n == 0:
        print(0)
        return
        
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, w = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        points.append((x, w))
    
    # Sort by x-coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    # For each point, calculate v_i = x_i + w_i and u_i = x_i - w_i
    v = []
    u = []
    for x, w in points:
        v.append(x + w)
        u.append(x - w)
    
    # We need to find the maximum clique size
    # Use coordinate compression for v values
    v_coords = sorted(set(v))
    v_to_idx = {val: idx for idx, val in enumerate(v_coords)}
    
    # Create a Fenwick tree / segment tree for maximum queries
    # Since we need range maximum queries, we'll use a segment tree
    
    # For indices from 0 to len(v)-1
    size = len(v_coords)
    
    # Segmented tree for maximum values
    seg_tree = [0] * (4 * size)
    
    def update(pos, val, node=0, left=0, right=size-1):
        if left == right:
            seg_tree[node] = max(seg_tree[node], val)
            return
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if pos <= mid:
            update(pos, val, 2*node+1, left, mid)
        else:
            update(pos, val, 2*node+2, mid+1, right)
        seg_tree[node] = max(seg_tree[2*node+1], seg_tree[2*node+2])
    
    def query(val, node=0, left=0, right=size-1):
        if right < 0:
            return 0
        if val < v_coords[0]:
            return 0
        if left > val:
            return 0
        if left == right:
            return seg_tree[node]
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if val <= mid:
            return query(val, 2*node+1, left, mid)
        else:
            return query(val, 2*node+2, mid+1, right)
    
    # Wait, this query implementation is wrong. I need to query by index.
    # Let me fix this.
    
    # Actually, let me do a simpler dynamic programming with coordinate compression.
    # For each point i, I want to find max dp[j] for j < i where v[j] <= u[i]
    
    # I'll rebuild the segment tree correctly.
    
    seg_tree = [0] * (4 * size)
    
    def update(pos, val, node=0, left=0, right=size-1):
        if left == right:
            seg_tree[node] = max(seg_tree[node], val)
            return
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if pos <= mid:
            update(pos, val, 2*node+1, left, mid)
        else:
            update(pos, val, 2*node+2, mid+1, right)
        seg_tree[node] = max(seg_tree[2*node+1], seg_tree[2*node+2])
    
    def query(q_val, node=0, left=0, right=size-1):
        # Query maximum value at positions [0, idx] where idx is the largest index such that v_coords[idx] <= q_val
        if left > right:
            return 0
        idx = -1
        for i, val in enumerate(v_coords):
            if val <= q_val:
                idx = i
            else:
                break
        if idx == -1:
            return 0
        # Actually, I need to query the maximum value in the range [0, idx]
        # But my segment tree query should be over the compressed values
        if left == right:
            return seg_tree[node]
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if idx <= mid:
            return query(q_val, 2*node+1, left, mid)
        else:
            return max(query(q_val, 2*node+2, mid+1, right), seg_tree[2*node+1]) if seg_tree[2*node+1] > 0 else query(q_val, 2*node+2, mid+1, right)
    
    # This is getting too complex. Let me use a simpler approach with sorting.
    
    # Final insight: we can use coordinate compression and then iterate.
    
    # Rebuild with correct segment tree for range maximum query from 0 to idx
    
    # Actually, let me use a different approach - just use the fact that the constraints are on v[j] <= u[i]
    
    # We'll process points from left to right
    # For each point i, we want max{dp[j] for j < i and v[j] <= u[i]}
    
    # Use coordinate compression of v values
    v_vals = sorted(set(v))
    v_to_idx = {val: idx for idx, val in enumerate(v_vals)}
    
    # Segment tree for range maximum query
    size_tree = len(v_vals)
    seg_tree = [0] * (4 * size_tree)
    
    def update(idx, val, node=0, left=0, right=size_tree-1):
        if left == right:
            seg_tree[node] = max(seg_tree[node], val)
            return
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if idx <= mid:
            update(idx, val, 2*node+1, left, mid)
        else:
            update(idx, val, 2*node+2, mid+1, right)
        seg_tree[node] = max(seg_tree[2*node+1], seg_tree[2*node+2])
    
    def query(l, r, node=0, left=0, right=size_tree-1):
        if left > r or right < l:
            return 0
        if left >= l and right <= r:
            return seg_tree[node]
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        res = 0
        if l <= mid:
            res = max(res, query(l, r, 2*node+1, left, mid))
        if r > mid:
            res = max(res, query(l, r, 2*node+2, mid+1, right))
        return res
    
    dp = [1] * n
    # Process each point
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the maximum dp[j] for j < i such that v[j] <= u[i]
        # First, find the largest v value that is <= u[i]
        # Since v_vals is sorted, we can binary search
        # Find the rightmost index such that v_vals[idx] <= u[i]
        left_idx, right_idx = 0, size_tree - 1
        pos = -1
        while left_idx <= right_idx:
            mid = (left_idx + right_idx) // 2
            if v_vals[mid] <= u[i]:
                pos = mid
                left_idx = mid + 1
            else:
                right_idx = mid - 1
        
        if pos == -1:
            max_prev = 0
        else:
            max_prev = query(0, pos, 0, 0, size_tree-1)
        
        dp[i] = max_prev + 1
    
    print(max(dp))

main()
```
